#10640. 8 Checks Developers Should Add Before Importing PDF Tables into an Application

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A PDF table can look correct and still produce unreliable application data. A wrapped description becomes two rows. An account code loses its leading zeros. A repeated page header is imported as a transaction. A missing decimal separator changes an amount without triggering an obvious error.

These problems are easy to overlook when an exported spreadsheet opens successfully. But opening a workbook proves very little about whether its contents belong in a database.

For developers building reporting dashboards, inventory systems, or document-import features, PDF extraction should feed a validation workflow. The following eight checks help separate readable output from data your application can safely process.

The examples use fictional invoice-style records and Python. They demonstrate a narrow validation contract rather than a complete PDF conversion service.

Not all PDFs contain the same kind of information.

A digitally generated document may include selectable text and vector lines. A scanned document may contain only page images. A mixed document can contain both, sometimes with an existing OCR layer that is incomplete or inaccurate.

Inspect representative pages before committing to an extraction strategy. Check whether text is selectable, whether copied Check parsing and validation behavior against fixtures

The pdfplumber documentation explains its text and table capabilities, including visual debugging. Its strengths are primarily associated with machine-generated PDFs; it is not an OCR engine.

OCRmyPDF adds a searchable text layer to scans. That does not, by itself, reconstruct a correct table schema. You still need to determine which text belongs in each row and column.

Practical check: Build a sample set containing a clean digital PDF, a scan, and a multi-page table before evaluating the extraction path.

A conversion step should create an inspectable intermediate result. It should not automatically authorize database writes.

For workflows that need a spreadsheet as the intermediate format, Lido is one option for converting PDF content to Excel. Evaluate the output against representative documents, then apply the same schema and reconciliation checks you would use with a custom extraction script.

Commit accepted records to the application.

This structure lets you replace an extraction tool without changing every downstream rule. It also provides a place to inspect failures before they become production records.

SitePoint’s guide to parsing spreadsheet data with Python provides background on working with spreadsheet formats in an application workflow.

Practical check: The importer should be able to reject a workbook even when conversion completed successfully.

Column names alone are not a sufficient contract. A field called “Date” could represent an invoice date, payment date, or service date.

Decide how repeated headers, subtotal rows, footnotes, and blank lines will be identified. Do not let them disappear through undocumented cleanup rules.

Load a workbook with deliberate type handling

The pandas read_excel reference documents these loading options. Using strings limits unwanted interpretation at this stage, while keep_default_na=False preserves values that would otherwise match default missing-value markers.

However, reading a cell as text cannot recover information already lost upstream. If a converter stored 00127 as the number 127, this setting will not reconstruct the original ID.

Practical check: Compare identifiers against the original document, including examples with leading zeros.

Removing every comma and currency symbol may look convenient, but it hides ambiguity. The strings follow different conventions.

Choose an explicit format per document family. The following parser accepts a deliberately narrow format: ASCII digits, optional correctly grouped thousands commas, a decimal point, and up to two decimal places. It permits a leading minus sign for adjustments.

This intentionally rejects parentheses, currency symbols, exponent notation, and comma-decimal formatting. Those formats may be valid in other documents, but supporting them requires an explicit rule.

Python’s Decimal documentation explains decimal arithmetic. Constructing a Decimal directly from the validated string avoids first introducing a binary floating-point representation.

Practical check: Keep the original extracted value alongside the normalized value, and reconcile the result against other evidence.

A hundred extracted rows are not necessarily the correct hundred rows. One line may be missing while another is duplicated.

Required fields are present and nonempty.

Dates are valid under the declared format.

Line identifiers are unique within the correct scope.

Recognized line-item totals reconcile with the appropriate subtotal.

Each accepted row has a traceable source location.

For a document whose stated subtotal is exactly the sum of its line amounts:

Do not compare that subtotal directly with a grand total containing tax, shipping, or other adjustments. First model those components.

Avoid a generic tolerance such as “accept differences below one dollar.” If the document requires rounding, define where rounding occurs, the currency precision, and the permitted discrepancy.

SitePoint’s overview of data preprocessing provides additional context for separating cleaning, transformation, and integration.

Practical check: Inject one missing row and one duplicate into your fixtures. Confirm that the workflow detects both.

When a record fails validation, a reviewer should not have to search a long PDF without context.

Keep a reference to the source document, page number, extraction run, and original field value. Where the extractor exposes cell coordinates, retain them so a review interface can highlight the relevant region.

Distinguish “not extracted,” “blank in source,” and “not applicable.” Turning all three into an empty string makes it harder to understand what happened.

Confidence scores can help prioritize work, but they are not interchangeable across tools and do not replace business validation. A highly confident extraction can still be placed in the wrong column.

Practical check: Ask someone unfamiliar with the document to resolve a flagged record using only the review interface and its source reference.

An import may stop after extraction, during validation, or while writing records. Retrying should not create another copy of every accepted line.

Assign each import a stable identity. A document hash can help identify byte-identical uploads, while an explicit tenant or account scope prevents unrelated users’ imports from being treated as one.

A hash alone is insufficient for every duplicate policy. Two differently encoded PDFs may represent the same invoice, and one file may legitimately need reprocessing after a parser correction.

Track both the document identity and processing version. Separate extraction attempts from committed business records.

At the database boundary, define the appropriate uniqueness rule and use a transaction for the unit you promise to import atomically. For example, invoice lines might be unique by account, invoice identity, and line number—not by invoice ID alone across all customers.

When reprocessing changes existing values, make that an explicit replacement or correction workflow rather than an unnoticed side effect of retrying.

Practical check: Run the same accepted import twice and verify that the application record count remains unchanged.

The most valuable fixtures are not always the most visually complicated. A tiny change can alter business meaning while producing valid-looking data.

Use pytest parameterization to exercise numeric-format boundarie. SitePoint’s Python unit-testing guide explains the broader testing workflow.

Parser tests do not verify the extraction engine. Add end-to-end fixtures with manually verified expected records for repeated headers, wrapped descriptions, scans, and multi-page tables.

Measure field accuracy and the number of documents that pass all required checks. A strong average can conceal a weak result on the one field your application depends on most.

Practical check: Retain a regression fixture for every meaningful production failure, using synthetic or appropriately sanitized documents.

PDF conversion produces candidate data. Your application decides whether that data is complete, consistent, and suitable for use.

A dependable workflow identifies the source format, preserves extraction evidence, enforces explicit column rules, reconciles values, and makes retries predictable.

Start with one document family and a small verified fixture set. Expand only when you can explain both why a record was accepted and how a reviewer can investigate one that was rejected.

The job description makes it clear that the district expects results for that money. Applicants must have a proven record of winning football games, making playoff appearances and developing championship-caliber teams.

The position is also a full-time, 12-month role, with the district offering additional salary supplements to attract offensive and defensive coordinators.

#10635. This new chip could slash data center energy waste

As data centers consume more energy to support growing digital demands, engineers at the University of California San Diego have introduced a new chip design that could make powering graphics processing units (GPUs) more efficient. The innovation focuses on a key function in electronics: converting high voltages into the lower levels required by computing hardware. In laboratory testing, a prototype chip successfully performed this type of voltage conversion with high efficiency under conditions similar to those found in modern data centers.

The findings, published in Nature Communications, suggest the potential for smaller and more energy-efficient systems in advanced computing environments.

Rethinking DC-DC Converters for Modern Electronics

At the center of the new design is an improved version of a widely used component known as a DC-DC step-down converter. These converters are found in nearly all electronic devices and serve as a critical link between power sources and sensitive circuits. Their job is to take a high incoming voltage and reduce it to the exact level needed for safe operation.

In data centers, electricity is often distributed at 48 volts, while GPU processors typically require much lower voltages, usually between 1 and 5 volts. Efficiently managing this large voltage drop has become increasingly challenging as computing systems grow more powerful and compact.

Limits of Traditional Power Conversion Technology

Conventional step-down converters often struggle when dealing with large differences between input and output voltage. As that gap increases, efficiency drops and it becomes harder to supply enough current. Most existing designs rely on magnetic components such as inductors. While these components have been refined over many years, they are approaching their practical limits and are becoming harder to improve further.

"We've gotten so good at designing inductive converters that there's not really much room left to improve them to meet future needs," said study senior author Patrick Mercier, professor in the Department of Electrical and Computer Engineering at the UC San Diego Jacobs School of Engineering.

Exploring Piezoelectric Resonators as an Alternative

To move beyond these limitations, Mercier and his team, including first author Jae-Young Ko, an electrical and computer engineering Ph.D. student at UC San Diego, investigated a different approach using piezoelectric resonators. These small devices store and transfer energy through mechanical vibrations rather than magnetic fields.

Converters based on piezoelectric components could offer several advantages. They have the potential to be smaller, more energy dense, more efficient, and easier to manufacture at scale. "They have a lot of room to grow and have the potential to deliver better performance than anything that's come before them," Mercier said.

However, earlier versions of piezoelectric converters have had difficulty maintaining efficiency and delivering sufficient power when handling large voltage differences.

Hybrid Design Achieves High Efficiency and Power Output

To overcome these issues, the researchers created a hybrid converter that combines a piezoelectric resonator with small, commercially available capacitors arranged in a carefully designed configuration. This setup enables the system to handle larger voltage conversions more effectively.

The team incorporated this design into a prototype chip and tested its performance. The device successfully converted 48 volts down to 4.8 volts -- a level commonly required in data centers -- with a peak efficiency of 96.2 percent. It also delivered roughly four times more output current than previous piezoelectric-based designs.

This hybrid approach offers several benefits. It creates multiple pathways for energy to move through the system, reduces wasted power, and lessens the strain on the resonator. Together, these improvements enhance both efficiency and power delivery while only slightly increasing the chip's size.

Challenges and Next Steps for Real-World Use

Although the technology shows strong promise, it is still in the early stages of development. The researchers view it as an important step toward overcoming the constraints of current power conversion systems. Future efforts will focus on refining materials, improving circuit designs, and developing better packaging methods.

One challenge is that piezoelectric resonators physically vibrate, which means they cannot be attached to circuit boards using standard soldering techniques. New integration strategies will be needed to incorporate them into electronic systems, Mercier explained.

"Piezoelectric-based converters aren't quite ready to replace existing power converter technologies yet," Mercier added. "But they offer a trajectory for improvement. We need to continue to improve on multiple areas -- materials, circuits and packaging -- to make this technology ready for data center applications."

This project was supported in part by the Power Management Integration Center (PMIC), an Industry-University Cooperative Research Center (IUCRC) funded by the National Science Foundation (award number 2052809).

Materials provided by University of California - San Diego. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10631. Apple Watch now listens to in-person conversations & lets you rewind them

Apple is introducing a new AI feature that allows Apple Watch users to rewind in-person conversations and see what someone just said.

The feature, called Live Rewind, is part of Apple’s new Audio Intelligence technology, which began rolling out to developers with the iOS 27.3 beta on November 3.

First announced in September, the technology also lets users generate AI summaries of conversations, helping them remember key details without taking notes.

Live Rewind allows users to double-press their Apple Watch’s digital crown to see a text version of what was said during the previous 15 seconds.

For example, someone could use the feature to remember directions, check what a waiter said, or recall a name they missed during a conversation.

Users can also ask Siri questions about what was said or save the text for later. Otherwise, it automatically disappears after 30 seconds.

Apple is also introducing Siri Recaps, which uses AI to summarize conversations and highlight important details such as dates, deadlines, and other information.

Unlike Live Rewind, Siri Recaps can run automatically throughout the day. Users can choose when it operates, such as during work hours, and turn it off whenever they want.

The summaries can be saved to Apple’s Notes or Journal apps. Any that aren’t saved will automatically disappear within seven days.

The company says Siri Recaps does not save raw audio, identify individual speakers, or create complete transcripts of conversations.

Instead, audio is securely processed using the Apple Watch, a paired iPhone, and Apple’s Private Cloud Compute technology to generate summaries.

Live Rewind also plays a sound and displays an animation when activated to alert nearby people that the feature is being used.

Audio Intelligence includes two other features that can identify songs playing nearby and recognize important sounds such as alarms, sirens, and doorbells.

The technology is limited to the Apple Watch Series 12 and Apple Watch Ultra 4, paired with a compatible iPhone.

It’s currently available through the iOS 27.3 developer beta, with a public beta planned before the features become widely available.

#10630. “Giant superatoms” could finally solve quantum computing’s biggest problem

Researchers at Chalmers University of Technology in Sweden have introduced a new theoretical design for quantum systems based on what they call "giant superatoms." This concept offers a fresh way to protect, control, and share quantum information, potentially bringing scientists closer to building large-scale quantum computers.

Quantum computers are expected to transform fields like drug discovery and encryption by solving problems that are far beyond the reach of conventional machines. However, progress has been limited by a major challenge known as decoherence. This occurs when quantum bits, or qubits, lose their information due to interactions with their surroundings. Even small amounts of electromagnetic noise can disrupt the fragile quantum states needed for computation.

"Quantum systems are extraordinarily powerful but also extremely fragile. The key to making them useful is learning how to control their interaction with the surrounding environment," says Lei Du, postdoctoral researcher in applied quantum technology at Chalmers.

Lei Du is the lead author of a study that outlines this new type of quantum system. The design is built around giant superatoms, which combine several important features. These systems reduce decoherence, remain stable, and consist of multiple interconnected "atoms" that function together as a single unit.

Giant superatoms bring together two previously separate ideas in quantum physics: giant atoms and superatoms. While each has been studied on its own, this is the first time they have been merged into a single system. These structures behave like atoms but are not found in nature. Instead, they are engineered by scientists (see fact box below).

The idea of giant atoms was first introduced by researchers at Chalmers over a decade ago and is now widely used in the field. A giant atom is typically designed as a qubit (which is the smallest unit of quantum information). Unlike ordinary atoms, it connects to light or sound waves at multiple, physically separated points. This allows it to interact with its environment in several places at once, helping it preserve quantum information.

"Waves that leave one connection point can travel through the environment and return to affect the atom at another point -- similar to hearing an echo of your own voice before you've finished speaking. This self-interaction leads to highly beneficial quantum effects, reduces decoherence and gives the system a form of memory of past interactions," explains Anton Frisk Kockum, Associate Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Although giant atoms have improved understanding of quantum behavior, they have had limitations when it comes to entanglement. Entanglement allows multiple qubits to share a single quantum state and act as one coordinated system, which is essential for powerful quantum computers.

To overcome this limitation, the research team combined giant atoms with the concept of superatoms. A superatom consists of several natural atoms that share the same quantum state and behave collectively as one larger atom.

This combination is expected to make it easier to create complex quantum states needed for quantum communication, networks, and highly sensitive measurement systems.

"A giant superatom may be envisaged as multiple giant atoms working together as a single entity, exhibiting a non-local interaction between light and matter. This enables quantum information from multiple qubits to be stored and controlled within one unit, without the need for increasingly complex surrounding circuitry," explains Lei Du.

"Giant superatoms open the door to entirely new capabilities, giving us a powerful new toolbox. They allow us to control quantum information and create entanglement in ways that were previously extremely difficult, or even impossible," says Janine Splettstoesser, Professor of Applied Quantum Physics at Chalmers and co-author of the study.

Toward Scalable and Practical Quantum Systems

This work creates new possibilities for building quantum systems that are both scalable and reliable. The researchers plan to move from theory toward actually constructing these systems. Their design could also be integrated with other quantum technologies, serving as a building block for connecting different types of quantum platforms.

"There is currently strong interest in hybrid approaches, in which different quantum systems work together, because each has its own strengths," says Anton Frisk Kockum. "Our research shows that smart design can reduce the need for increasingly complex hardware and giant superatoms are bringing us one step closer to practically applicable quantum technology."

More on: Methods for protecting, controlling and distributing quantum information

The study shows that the way giant superatoms interact with light depends on their internal quantum states. This discovery gives researchers greater control over how quantum information moves through a system. They describe two different ways of connecting these structures to achieve useful outcomes.

In one setup, several giant superatoms are closely linked in a specific arrangement. This allows them to pass quantum states between each other without decoherence, meaning no information is lost.

In another setup, the atoms are spaced farther apart but connected in a carefully tuned way so that waves remain synchronized. This makes it possible to direct quantum signals and distribute entanglement over long distances.

Superatoms and giant atoms are engineered systems that behave like atoms rather than naturally occurring ones.

A superatom is a quantum system made up of multiple natural atoms that share a single quantum state and respond to light as one entity.

A giant atom, on the other hand, connects to light or sound waves at several separate points in space. It is called "giant" because it is larger than the wavelength of the light it interacts with.

Giant atoms have defined energy levels and follow the rules of quantum mechanics, yet they can reach sizes of up to millimeters, making them visible to the naked eye. Through electromagnetic or acoustic waves, they can interact with their surroundings at multiple locations at the same time. One way to picture this is as a single atom linked to a wave at several distant points. This unusual setup allows the atom to be influenced by the waves it produces.

Materials provided by Chalmers University of Technology. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10629. Beyond the Password Reset: Building Recovery Experiences That Protect Online Shopping

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

An unfamiliar delivery address appears in a shopper’s account. Minutes later, an order confirmation arrives for something they never purchased. They try to sign in, but their password no longer works.

For the consumer, this is an urgent problem: stop the misuse, recover access, and understand what happened. For developers, it exposes a gap between building a login screen and building a service that remains useful when that login is compromised.

This article examines recovery from a business-to-consumer perspective: what an individual shopper needs, and how developers can translate those needs into safer interfaces and backend controls.

Account takeover and identity theft can overlap, but they are different problems.

Account takeover means someone has gained unauthorized control of an existing account. Identity theft involves misuse of someone’s personal information. Recovering access to one shopping account does not necessarily resolve misuse elsewhere.

Design the interface around questions a worried customer can answer:

A recovery page should explain the scope of help. “Recover your shopping account” is a specific promise. “Restore your identity” describes a much broader outcome that an ordinary retailer cannot deliver on its own.

Before building the workflow, list the consumer-visible assets involved: account access, addresses, order history, payment references, contact details, and support messages. Each needs its own review or remediation step.

A shopper who cannot sign in must still be able to locate help. Provide a public “My account may be compromised” entry point alongside ordinary password recovery.

Separate the two journeys. Someone who forgot a password usually needs a credential reset. Someone reporting suspicious activity may also need unauthorized changes reversed, orders investigated, and existing access revoked.

Avoid using a submitted email address as proof of ownership. A public report can initiate triage, but it should not expose order history or authorize sensitive changes.

OWASP’s Forgot Password Cheat Sheet recommends consistent responses for existing and nonexistent accounts, rate limiting, and securely generated reset tokens that expire and work only once.

If the details match an account, we’ll send instructions through an available recovery method.

The frontend message is only one part of that protection. Different response timing or API behavior can still reveal account existence.

After verifying a recovery factor, resist granting immediate access to every account feature.

A useful architecture gives the consumer a short-lived recovery session with narrowly defined permissions. For example, it might permit reviewing security settings while preventing purchases, data exports, or changes to payment information.

This type documents intent; it does not enforce security. The server must validate the session, its expiry, its association with the account, and the required permission on every request.

Check resource ownership separately. Permission to request an order review must never allow a consumer to submit another customer’s order identifier.

Do not treat an attacker’s recently changed email address as trustworthy recovery evidence. Review recovery options against previously established evidence and the account’s required level of assurance.

A new password is insufficient if an attacker still holds a valid session.

OWASP’s compromise-recovery guidance calls for reviewing recovery contacts and authentication methods, removing unauthorized changes, and invalidating existing sessions and outstanding recovery credentials after successful recovery.

For a consumer application, translate that into a visible checklist:

Use a durable workflow so a partial failure cannot silently appear as completion. Credential replacement and session revocation may span different systems; record pending work, retry failed operations safely, and show the consumer an accurate status.

JWT-based applications need particular care. Changing a password does not automatically invalidate an independently verifiable token. Choose an enforceable revocation strategy, such as checking a server-side session record or account security version on protected requests.

The OWASP Session Management Cheat Sheet provides background on session lifecycle controls.

Recovery screens are often used on a phone, in a hurry, by someone who is uncertain about what happened.

Use short steps with clear outcomes: “Review recent changes,” “Secure your sign-in,” and “Check your orders.” Explain why information is requested before asking for it.

Use native form controls with visible labels. Associate field instructions and errors with their inputs, keep keyboard navigation predictable, and move focus deliberately when a step changes. SitePoint’s HTML Form Elements guide is useful background for building these controls.

A simple reporting form might begin with:

Provide an alternative when the consumer cannot access their usual inbox. That alternative needs its own verification process; support staff should not bypass security simply because a caller sounds convincing.

Once the immediate account problem is contained, a consumer may need help addressing misuse of personal information elsewhere.

Keep this guidance separate from the application’s own recovery controls. A retailer can investigate its orders and secure its accounts. It cannot directly correct records held by unrelated organizations.

For U.S. consumers, link to the FTC’s IdentityTheft.gov as a government resource. Offer country-appropriate resources when serving other regions.

Consumers considering paid assistance can also review an identity recovery service to understand the support available for broader restoration tasks. They should check eligibility, exclusions, and service terms before relying on it.

External assistance should remain optional. Restoring access to a customer’s account should not depend on purchasing another product.

Consumers need an understandable record of what happened. Offer a case summary with relevant timestamps, reported changes, affected orders, completed actions, and unresolved questions.

Separate customer-facing information from internal investigation details. A consumer may need to know that an address changed; they do not need raw authentication tokens or internal fraud-detection rules.

Treat recovery data as sensitive. Avoid recording passwords, recovery secrets, or full payment details in logs. Restrict staff access to case information and define retention periods appropriate to its purpose.

If evidence uploads are necessary, provide a secured upload route with access controls and a deletion policy. Do not ask customers to send sensitive documents to an ordinary support inbox by default.

A successful redirect is a weak definition of recovery.

After recovery, help consumers strengthen their sign-in. SitePoint’s Developer Guide to Implementing Passkeys offers a starting point for exploring passkey authentication.

A stronger sign-in method still needs a carefully designed fallback. Evaluate the recovery path whenever you change authentication.

For a shopper, recovery succeeds when unauthorized access stops, legitimate access returns, and the next steps are clear.

Developers can support that outcome by treating recovery as a complete product journey: limited permissions, enforceable revocation, accessible interfaces, accurate case status, and clear boundaries around external assistance.

The most useful question in a design review is simple: if this were your account, could you regain control and tell what still needs attention?

#10625. This simple change stops robot swarms from getting stuck

Imagine a swarm of robots rushing to complete an urgent job, such as cleaning up an oil spill or assembling complex machinery. At first, adding more robots speeds things up. But after a certain point, the space becomes crowded, robots start interfering with one another, and overall progress slows.

This raises a simple but important question: in a limited area, how many robots can you deploy before efficiency starts to drop? Researchers at Harvard believe they have found a clear answer.

A new study from the lab of L. Mahadevan, the Lola England de Valpine Professor of Applied Mathematics, Organismic and Evolutionary Biology, and Physics, shows that adding a controlled amount of randomness to how robots move can reduce congestion and improve performance in crowded environments.

The work combines mathematical modeling, computer simulations, and real-world experiments. It demonstrates how basic local movement rules can lead to organized, efficient outcomes on a larger scale. The findings could influence how robotic fleets are designed and may even apply to human crowd management and traffic flow. The research was published in Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences and led by applied mathematics Ph.D. student Lucy Liu, with guidance from SEAS Senior Research Fellow Justin Werfel.

Why Randomness Helps Predict Complex Behavior

Studying dense crowds is difficult because individuals can take countless possible paths and interact in unpredictable ways, Liu explained. To simplify the problem, the researchers treated each robot as a basic unit with a small, adjustable amount of variation in its movement.

"This might be counterintuitive, because how could randomness make things easier to work with?" said Liu. "But in this case, when you have a lot of randomness, it becomes possible to take averages -- average distances, average times, average behaviors. This makes it a lot easier to make predictions."

To explore this idea, the team created computer simulations of robot groups, referred to as agents. Each agent started at a random location and was assigned a random destination. Once it reached its target, it immediately received a new one, mimicking continuous task assignment in real-world systems.

Each agent moved toward its goal with a tunable amount of variation, described as "noise." With no noise, agents moved in straight lines. With high noise, their paths became erratic and inefficient. However, this wandering also helped them navigate around one another.

The simulations revealed a clear pattern. When agents moved in perfectly straight paths, they quickly formed dense clusters and traffic jams that halted progress. When movement became too random, congestion disappeared but efficiency dropped due to excessive wandering.

Between these extremes, the researchers identified a sweet spot. In this range, agents occasionally bumped into one another and formed short-lived clusters, but still managed to slip past and keep moving. This balance allowed the system to maintain a steady flow.

Using these insights, the team developed formulas to estimate "goal attainment rate," or how many destinations are reached over time. These equations made it possible to determine the ideal combination of crowd density and movement randomness to maximize performance.

To confirm their findings, Liu collaborated with physicist Federico Toschi at Eindhoven University of Technology in the Netherlands. Together, they set up experiments with small wheeled robots in a lab equipped with an overhead camera.

Each robot carried a QR code so its position could be tracked and updated with new destinations. Although the physical robots moved more slowly and less precisely than the simulated agents, they displayed the same overall patterns.

The experiments supported a key idea: highly complex coordination does not require advanced intelligence or centralized control. Instead, simple local rules can produce effective group behavior, at least within certain density limits.

"Understanding how active matter, whether it is a swarm of ants, a herd of animals, or a group of robots, become functional and execute tasks in crowded environments using the principles of self-organization, is relevant to many questions in behavioral ecology," Mahadevan said. "Our study suggests strategies that might well be much broader than the instantiation we have focused on."

Liu noted that she has long been interested in designing safer and more efficient crowded spaces. This research points toward a future where the movement of large groups, whether robots, vehicles, or people, could be predicted and optimized using mathematical tools.

The results suggest that introducing controlled variability into movement patterns may improve flow in many real-world systems, from factory floors to city streets.

Funding for the research came from the National Science Foundation Graduate Research Fellowship Program under Grant No. DGE 2140743, along with grants from the Simons Foundation and the Henri Seydoux Fund.

Materials provided by Harvard John A. Paulson School of Engineering and Applied Sciences. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10622. Brazil football match sets record with 51 red cards after massive brawl involving chairs & stretchers

A football match in Brazil descended into absolute chaos after a massive brawl involving players, coaches, and even ball boys resulted in a staggering 51 red cards, with police later called to the stadium.

The incident occurred on November 1 during an Under-20 championship semifinal between Uberlândia and Mamoré at the Parque do Sabiá Stadium in Minas Gerais.

Mamoré was leading 3-1 deep into stoppage time when a goal celebration sparked a confrontation that quickly spiraled out of control.

According to Brazilian outlet O Tempo, Mamoré player Davi Fernandes Bittencourt made provocative gestures and shouted insults toward the opposing bench.

Uberlândia’s goalkeeping coach, Paulo Sérgio Dias dos Santos, then reportedly stormed onto the field and pushed Bittencourt, triggering an all-out brawl that looked more like something out of a hockey game than a football match.

Players and coaching staff began exchanging punches and kicks, with footage showing chairs and even stretchers being thrown during the violence.

The referee’s report also claimed that six ball boys joined the fighting, attacking players and staff with punches, kicks, and thrown objects.

Referee Fernando Patrick Drosghic Modesto ultimately abandoned the match, recording 48 additional dismissals in his official report because the chaos prevented him from showing the cards on the field.

As ge.globo reported, three players had already been sent off before the brawl, bringing the final total to 51.

Of those dismissed, 43 were players and eight were members of the coaching staffs.

“Upset” man warned by police after kicking 9-year-old player during football game

Soccer match goes viral after loose ball flies into traffic and causes crash

Richard Lewis: Gaules’ actions lead to harassment and it needs to stop

The trouble didn’t end on the pitch, either.

Military police were called after the match following reports of vandalism in the visiting team’s locker room.

Officers discovered approximately 14 damaged doors, along with broken sinks, toilets, showers, electrical installations, and furniture. Two hot tubs were also reportedly damaged. No arrests were made.

Remarkably, the match had already been suspended for around 40 minutes earlier in the second half after Uberlândia officials confronted the referee over another dismissal. The official subsequently reported receiving threats.

The 51 dismissals comfortably surpass the previous Guinness World Record of 36 red cards, set in 2011 during an Argentine fifth-division match between Atlético Claypole and Victoriano Arenas.

A post shared by TMC Esporte (@tmcesporte)

However, Guinness has not yet officially updated its record to recognize the Brazilian incident.

It’s also not the first time football has produced bizarre scenes this year. In March, masked fans sabotaged a VAR monitor during a German match, while streamer IShowSpeed was forced to leave an Algerian football game after spectators began throwing objects at him.

Uberlândia and Mamoré are scheduled to meet again on November 8, although the unprecedented number of dismissals could create major complications for both teams.

#10620. Top 5 3D Animation Software in 2026

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Choosing the best 3D animation software depends on your experience, budget, and creative goals. Some tools offer complete 3D modeling and animation workflows, while others specialize in professional character animation, visual effects, or AI-powered video creation. This guide compares five options and notes how each fits into web and game projects, so designers and developers can see which one suits their pipeline.

Blender is one of the strongest free options for anyone looking for comprehensive 3D animation software. This open-source application combines modeling, sculpting, rigging, animation, rendering, simulation, and compositing in one package.

Blender provides a broad production workflow without requiring users to pay for a standard commercial license. Its active community, learning resources, and add-on ecosystem make it accessible to independent creators. It is suitable for everything from simple animated scenes to complex short films and game assets.

For web and game developers: Blender ships with a glTF 2.0 importer and exporter, and glTF is the format libraries such as three.js load for 3D on the web. See the Blender manual’s glTF page for export options.

Its extensive interface and feature set can feel overwhelming to new users. Learning advanced rigging, lighting, materials, and rendering takes time, and some established studios may have specialized pipelines built around other applications.

Beginners, freelancers, indie game developers, students, and professionals who need a powerful 3D animation tool without a software subscription.

Autodesk Maya is a professional 3D application widely associated with character animation, rigging, film production, and game development. It offers detailed control over character movement and complex animation workflows, making it a strong choice for artists working in established production environments.

Maya provides sophisticated tools for animating characters and managing complex scenes. Its established role in many professional workflows makes it a valuable skill for artists pursuing careers in film, television, and game development. It also offers detailed control over animation timing, character motion, and rig behavior.

Maya generally requires a paid subscription, which can be expensive for hobbyists and independent creators. Its advanced interface also takes time to learn, particularly for users who have never worked with professional 3D software.

Professional animators, film and television artists, game studios, and users who need advanced character animation and rigging capabilities.

Cinema 4D is a 3D modeling, animation, and rendering application known for its motion graphics workflow. It is widely used for commercial visuals, broadcast graphics, product animations, and creative work that combines 3D elements with graphic design.

Cinema 4D is a strong option for designers who want to create polished motion graphics and branded animation. Its MoGraph workflow can make repetitive or complex movements easier to manage, and its integration with common design workflows is helpful for agencies and creative teams.

For web and product designers: Cinema 4D includes a glTF exporter, which helps when a designer needs to hand a 3D model to a developer for the web. Maxon documents the options on its glTF export settings page.

Cinema 4D is a commercial product, so ongoing licensing costs may be a concern. Although it can handle a wide range of 3D work, users focused on highly specialized character animation or procedural visual effects may prefer tools designed around those workflows.

Motion designers, advertising agencies, broadcast artists, product visualization specialists, and creators who regularly produce animated graphics.

Houdini, developed by SideFX, is a 3D software package known for procedural workflows and advanced visual effects. Rather than relying entirely on manually created steps, artists can build systems that generate and modify complex scenes, simulations, and effects.

Houdini offers exceptional flexibility for technical artists and visual-effects specialists. Procedural workflows make it possible to create reusable systems and adjust complex effects efficiently. Its capabilities are particularly valuable when a project needs detailed simulations or large numbers of interconnected elements.

For game developers: SideFX offers Houdini Engine plugins for Unity and Unreal Engine, which let procedural Houdini assets be used inside those engines. SideFX explains the plugins in its Houdini Engine FAQ.

Houdini has a steep learning curve, especially for beginners who are unfamiliar with node-based workflows. Building effective procedural systems requires patience and technical understanding. While free learning options are available, some commercial uses and advanced workflows require an appropriate paid license.

Visual-effects artists, technical directors, simulation specialists, and experienced 3D creators working on demanding film, television, or game projects.

MagicLight.AI is different from the four tools above. It is an AI-powered video creation platform rather than a traditional 3D application, so it produces finished animated videos, not editable 3D scenes, rigs, or game assets. It is included here for readers who want animated storytelling without building a 3D pipeline. It supports AI-generated characters, scene creation, storyboarding, and voice features.

MagicLight.AI can reduce the time and technical effort needed to create animated videos. It is particularly useful for content creators, educators, marketers, and storytellers who want to focus on the script and final message rather than mastering a complex 3D production pipeline.

MagicLight.AI is not a replacement for dedicated 3D software when you need precise control over mesh topology, character rigs, keyframes, physics, or custom 3D scenes. Creators who need editable 3D assets for games or studio production will need a conventional 3D application such as the ones above.

Content creators, educators, and marketers who want to produce animated videos efficiently without learning a traditional 3D animation workflow.

The right software is the one that supports your actual production needs. Before choosing a tool, consider the following factors.

If you want to model objects, build character rigs, and control animation precisely, start with a dedicated 3D application such as Blender or Maya. If your goal is to create animated storytelling videos from scripts or ideas with less manual production work, an AI video tool such as MagicLight.AI may be a more convenient option.

Blender is an excellent starting point if you need a complete 3D toolset without a standard license fee. Houdini also has a free Apprentice edition with restrictions. Maya and Cinema 4D are commercial applications, so compare their current plans against your expected usage before committing.

Different applications suit different workflows. Blender is versatile, Maya excels at character animation, Cinema 4D is well suited to motion graphics, and Houdini is especially powerful for procedural effects. MagicLight.AI is better suited to AI-assisted video production than to detailed, manually controlled 3D scene creation.

Beginners may find Blender’s extensive learning resources helpful, although its broad feature set takes time to master. Maya and Houdini demand more specialized skills for advanced work, while Cinema 4D is often considered by motion designers seeking a more direct design-oriented workflow. AI video tools can reduce the amount of technical setup required, but still require creative direction and review.

Think about where the finished animation will be used. A web project may need a lightweight glTF model. A game project may require exportable 3D assets, rig compatibility, and a game-engine workflow. A commercial motion graphic may depend on rendering and compositing integration. A social video or animated explainer may prioritize turnaround time, narration, subtitles, and scene continuity.

The best 3D animation software in 2026 depends on your budget, experience, and intended output. Blender is the strongest starting point for many users who want a comprehensive free 3D workflow. Autodesk Maya is a compelling option for professional character animation, Cinema 4D suits motion graphics and commercial visuals, and Houdini stands out for procedural effects and complex simulations. MagicLight.AI offers a different approach: it helps creators generate animated videos with AI rather than manually building a traditional 3D production pipeline.

Blender is one of the best free options because it includes modeling, rigging, animation, rendering, and other production tools in a single open-source application. It is suitable for beginners as well as experienced artists.

Yes. Free software can support professional-quality work when used effectively. Blender, for example, provides a comprehensive toolset for animation and rendering. The final result depends on the artist’s skills, project requirements, hardware, and production workflow.

Blender is a strong choice for beginners who want to learn traditional 3D modeling and animation without paying for a standard software license. Those who mainly want to create animated videos from stories or scripts may prefer exploring an AI video creation platform such as MagicLight.AI.

Blender and Cinema 4D can both export glTF, a format widely used for 3D on the web. Blender is free, so it is the easiest place to start.

#10619. Quantum AI just got shockingly good at predicting chaos

A new study led by researchers at UCL (University College London) shows that combining quantum computing with artificial intelligence can significantly improve predictions of complex physical systems over long periods. The hybrid approach outperforms leading models that rely only on conventional computers.

The results, published in Science Advances, could enhance simulations of how liquids and gases behave, known as fluid dynamics. These types of models are essential in fields such as climate science, transportation, medicine, and energy production.

The improved accuracy appears to come from how quantum computers process information. Unlike traditional computers that use bits set to either 1 or 0, quantum computers use qubits, which can exist as 1, 0, or anything in between. In addition, each qubit can influence others, allowing a relatively small number of qubits to represent an enormous number of possible states.

Professor Peter Coveney, senior author from UCL Chemistry and the Advanced Research Computing Centre, explained the challenge: "To make predictions about complex systems, we can either run a full simulation, which might take weeks -- often too long to be useful -- or we can use an AI model which is quicker but more unreliable over longer time scales.

"Our quantum-informed AI model means we could provide more accurate predictions quickly. Making predictions about fluid flow and turbulence is a fundamental science challenge but it also has many applications. Our method can be used in climate forecasting, in modeling blood flow and the interaction of molecules, or to better design wind farms so they generate more energy."

Although quantum computers are widely expected to surpass classical machines in power, their real-world use has so far been limited. This new approach integrates quantum computing into a specific stage of the AI training process.

Typically, AI models learn from large datasets generated by simulations or observations. In this case, the data is first processed by a quantum computer, which identifies key statistical patterns that remain stable over time. These patterns, known as invariant statistical properties, are then used to guide the training of an AI model running on a conventional supercomputer.

The quantum-informed AI system delivered about 20 percent greater accuracy compared to standard AI models that did not use quantum-derived patterns. It also maintained stable predictions over longer periods, even when modeling chaotic systems.

Another major advantage was efficiency. The method required hundreds of times less memory, making it far more practical for large-scale simulations.

This performance boost comes from two defining features of quantum computing. Entanglement allows qubits to influence each other regardless of distance, while superposition enables a qubit to exist in multiple states at once until measured. Together, these properties allow quantum systems to process vast amounts of information in a compact form.

Demonstrating Practical Quantum Advantage

First author Maida Wang of the UCL Centre for Computational Science said: "Our new method appears to demonstrate 'quantum advantage' in a practical way -- that is, the quantum computer outperforms what is possible through classical computing alone. These findings could inspire the development of novel classical approaches that achieve even higher accuracy, though they would likely lack the remarkable data compression and parameter efficiency offered by our method. The next steps are to scale up the method using larger datasets and to apply it to real-world situations which typically involve even more complexity. In addition, a provable theoretical framework will be proposed."

Co-first author Xiao Xue, from Advanced Research Computing at UCL, added: "In this work, we demonstrate for the first time that quantum computing can be meaningfully integrated with classical machine learning methods to tackle complex dynamical systems, including fluid mechanics. It is exciting to see this kind of 'quantum-informed' approach moving towards practical use."

The researchers suggest that quantum computers are particularly well suited to modeling these systems because they can compactly represent their underlying physics. Many complex systems behave in ways that resemble quantum effects, where changes in one region can influence distant parts of the system, similar to entanglement.

Overcoming Limits of Current Quantum Hardware

Current quantum computers face challenges such as noise, errors, and interference, which often require large numbers of measurements. The new method avoids these issues by using the quantum computer only once during the workflow, rather than repeatedly exchanging data between quantum and classical systems.

The study used a 20-qubit IQM quantum computer connected to powerful classical computing resources at the Leibniz Supercomputing Centre in Germany.

To function, quantum computers must operate at extremely low temperatures, around minus 273C (close to absolute zero, colder than anything in space).

The research was funded by UCL and the UK's Engineering and Physical Sciences Research Council (EPSRC), with additional support from IQM Quantum Computers and the Leibniz Supercomputing Centre in Munich.

As researchers continue to scale up this approach, it could open the door to more accurate and efficient predictions across a wide range of scientific and engineering applications.

Materials provided by University College London. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10617. Dragon Ball Z composer elected Texas Attorney General

A former Dragon Ball Z composer has been elected Texas Attorney General, marking an unlikely career transformation from creating music for Goku and Vegeta to becoming the state’s top lawyer.

Democrat Nathan Johnson defeated Republican Mayes Middleton in the November 3 election, marking the first time in 32 years that a Democrat has been elected Texas Attorney General, with Dan Morales being the last to win the office in 1994.

Johnson, who has served in the Texas Senate since 2019, will take office in January 2027, replacing outgoing Attorney General Ken Paxton.

While his election is a major political upset, anime fans might recognize Johnson’s name from an entirely different career.

Before entering politics, Johnson worked as a composer for Funimation’s English dub of Dragon Ball Z, providing music for the first 67 episodes of the 2005 Ultimate Uncut edition.

He also contributed music to five Dragon Ball Z movies, including Broly: Second Coming, Fusion Reborn, and Wrath of the Dragon.

In a 2012 interview with The Dao of Dragon Ball, Johnson revealed that he had never watched the anime before being hired and initially struggled to connect with its characters and story.

However, he eventually developed an appreciation for the series, particularly through Gohan, whose relationship with his father resonated with Johnson as a parent.

His connection to Dragon Ball Z even became part of his political identity when he first ran for the Texas Senate in 2018.

Speaking to The Dao of Dragon Ball following that election victory, Johnson revealed that younger voters were surprised to discover his anime background.

“Along the way some of them found out about my music background, and specifically about Dragon Ball Z, and they got really excited about it,” he explained.

#10612. Think AI "knows" what it’s doing? Scientists say think again

These are everyday words people use to describe what goes on in the human mind. But when those same terms are applied to artificial intelligence, they can unintentionally make machines seem more human than they really are.

"We use mental verbs all the time in our daily lives, so it makes sense that we might also use them when we talk about machines -- it helps us relate to them," said Jo Mackiewicz, professor of English at Iowa State. "But at the same time, when we apply mental verbs to machines, there's also a risk of blurring the line between what humans and AI can do."

Mackiewicz and Jeanine Aune, a teaching professor of English and director of the advanced communication program at Iowa State, are part of a research team that studied how writers describe AI using human-like language. This type of wording, known as anthropomorphism, assigns human traits to non-human systems. Their study, "Anthropomorphizing Artificial Intelligence: A Corpus Study of Mental Verbs Used with AI and ChatGPT," was published in Technical Communication Quarterly.

The research team also included Matthew J. Baker, associate professor of linguistics at Brigham Young University, and Jordan Smith, assistant professor of English at the University of Northern Colorado. Both previously studied at Iowa State University.

Why Human-Like Language About AI Can Be Misleading

According to the researchers, using mental verbs to describe AI can create a false impression. Words such as "think," "know," "understand," and "want" suggest that a system has thoughts, intentions, or awareness. In reality, AI does not possess beliefs or feelings. It produces responses by analyzing patterns in data, not by forming ideas or making conscious decisions.

Mackiewicz and Aune also pointed out that this kind of language can overstate what AI is capable of. Phrases like "AI decided" or "ChatGPT knows" can make systems seem more independent or intelligent than they actually are. This can lead to unrealistic expectations about how reliable or capable AI is.

There is also a broader concern. When AI is described as if it has intentions, it can distract from the humans behind it. Developers, engineers, and organizations are responsible for how these systems are built and used.

"Certain anthropomorphic phrases may even stick in readers' minds and can potentially shape public perception of AI in unhelpful ways," Aune said.

How News Writers Actually Use AI Language

To better understand how often this kind of language appears, the researchers analyzed the News on the Web (NOW) corpus. This massive dataset contains more than 20 billion words from English-language news articles published in 20 countries.

They focused on how frequently mental verbs such as "learns," "means," and "knows" were used alongside terms like AI and ChatGPT.

Mental Verbs Are Less Common Than Expected

The study found that news writers do not frequently pair AI-related terms with mental verbs.

While anthropomorphism is common in everyday speech, it appears far less often in news writing. "Anthropomorphism has been shown to be common in everyday speech, but we found there's far less usage in news writing," Mackiewicz said.

Among the examples identified, the word "needs" appeared most often with AI, showing up 661 times. For ChatGPT, "knows" was the most frequent pairing, but it appeared only 32 times.

The researchers noted that editorial standards may play a role. Associated Press guidelines, which discourage attributing human emotions or traits to AI, could be influencing how journalists write about these technologies.

Context Matters More Than the Words Themselves

Even when mental verbs were used, they were not always anthropomorphic.

For instance, the word "needs" often described basic requirements rather than human-like qualities. Phrases such as "AI needs large amounts of data" or "AI needs some human assistance" are similar to how people describe non-human systems like cars or recipes. In these cases, the language does not imply that AI has thoughts or desires.

In other cases, "needs" was used to express what should be done, such as "AI needs to be trained" or "AI needs to be implemented." Aune explained that these examples were often written in passive voice, which shifts responsibility back to human actors rather than the technology itself.

The study also showed that not all uses of mental verbs are equal. Some phrases move closer to suggesting human-like qualities.

For example, statements like "AI needs to understand the real world" can imply expectations tied to human reasoning, ethics, or awareness. These uses go beyond simple descriptions and begin to suggest deeper capabilities.

"These instances showed that anthropomorphizing isn't all-or-nothing and instead exists on a spectrum," Aune said

Overall, the researchers found that anthropomorphism in news coverage is both less frequent and more nuanced than many might assume.

"Overall, our analysis shows that anthropomorphization of AI in news writing is far less common -- and far more nuanced -- than we might think," Mackiewicz said. "Even the instances that did anthropomorphize AI varied widely in strength."

The findings highlight the importance of context. Simply counting words is not enough to understand how language shapes meaning.

"For writers, this nuance matters: the language we choose shapes how readers understand AI systems, their capabilities and the humans responsible for them," Mackiewicz said.

The research team also emphasized that these insights can help professionals think more carefully about how they describe AI in their work.

"Our findings can help technical and professional communication practitioners reflect on how they think about AI technologies as tools in their writing process and how they write about AI," the research team wrote in the published study.

As AI continues to develop, the way people talk about it will remain important. Mackiewicz and Aune said writers will need to stay mindful of how word choices influence perception.

Looking ahead, the team suggested that future studies could explore how different words shape understanding and whether even rare uses of anthropomorphic language have a strong impact on how people view AI.

Materials provided by Iowa State University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10609. Artificial neurons successfully communicate with living brain cells

Engineers at Northwestern University have created printed artificial neurons that go beyond imitation and can directly interact with real brain cells. These flexible, low-cost devices produce electrical signals that closely resemble those generated by living neurons, allowing them to activate biological brain tissue.

In experiments using slices of mouse brain, the artificial neurons successfully triggered responses in real neurons. This result shows a new level of compatibility between electronic devices and living neural systems.

Toward Brain Interfaces and Energy-Efficient AI

This advance moves researchers closer to electronics that can directly interface with the nervous system. Potential uses include brain-machine interfaces and neuroprosthetics, such as implants that could help restore hearing, vision, or movement.

The technology also points toward a new generation of computing systems inspired by the brain. By replicating how neurons communicate, future hardware could perform complex tasks using far less energy. The brain remains the most energy-efficient computing system known, and scientists hope to apply its principles to modern technology.

The study will be published on April 15 in the journal Nature Nanotechnology.

"The world we live in today is dominated by artificial intelligence (AI)," said Northwestern's Mark C. Hersam, who led the study. "The way you make AI smarter is by training it on more and more data. This data-intensive training leads to a massive power-consumption problem. Therefore, we have to come up with more efficient hardware to handle big data and AI. Because the brain is five orders of magnitude more energy efficient than a digital computer, it makes sense to look to the brain for inspiration for next-generation computing."

Hersam is an expert in brain-inspired computing and holds multiple roles at Northwestern University, including the Walter P. Murphy Professor of Materials Science and Engineering at the McCormick School of Engineering. He also is a professor of medicine at Northwestern University Feinberg School of Medicine and a professor of chemistry at the Weinberg College of Arts and Sciences. In addition, he serves as chair of the department of materials science and engineering, director of the Materials Research Science and Engineering Center, and a member of the International Institute for Nanotechnology. He co-led the study with Vinod K. Sangwan, a research associate professor at McCormick.

Why the Brain Outperforms Traditional Silicon

Modern computers handle increasing workloads by packing billions of identical transistors onto rigid, two-dimensional silicon chips. Each component behaves the same way, and once manufactured, the system remains fixed.

The brain works very differently. It consists of many types of neurons, each with specialized roles, arranged in soft, three-dimensional networks. These networks are constantly changing, forming and adjusting connections as learning occurs.

"Silicon achieves complexity by having billions of identical devices," Hersam said. "Everything is the same, rigid and fixed once it's fabricated. The brain is the opposite. It's heterogeneous, dynamic and three-dimensional. To move in that direction, we need new materials and new ways to build electronics."

Although artificial neurons have been developed before, most produce overly simple signals. To achieve more complex behavior, engineers typically need large networks of devices, which increases energy use.

Printable Materials Enable Brain-Like Behavior

To better replicate real neural activity, Hersam's team built artificial neurons using soft, printable materials that more closely match the brain's structure. Their approach relies on electronic inks made from nanoscale flakes of molybdenum disulfide (MoS2), which acts as a semiconductor, and graphene, which serves as an electrical conductor. These materials were deposited onto flexible polymer surfaces using aerosol jet printing.

Previously, researchers treated the polymer in these inks as a flaw because it interfered with electrical performance. As a result, they removed it after printing. In this work, the team used that same feature to enhance the device.

"Instead of fully removing the polymer, we partially decompose it," he said. "Then, when we pass current through the device, we drive further decomposition of the polymer. This decomposition occurs in a spatially inhomogeneous manner, leading to formation of a conductive filament, such that all the current is constricted into a narrow region in space."

That narrow conductive path produces a sudden electrical response similar to a neuron firing. The resulting device can generate a wide variety of signals, including single spikes, continuous firing, and bursting patterns, closely resembling real neural communication.

Because each artificial neuron can produce more complex signals, fewer components are needed to perform advanced tasks. This could significantly improve computing efficiency.

Testing Artificial Neurons on Real Brain Tissue

To evaluate whether the artificial neurons could truly interact with living systems, the researchers partnered with Indira M. Raman, the Bill and Gayle Cook Professor of Neurobiology at Weinberg. Her team applied the artificial signals to slices of mouse cerebellum.

The results showed that the electrical spikes matched key biological properties, including their timing and duration. These signals reliably activated real neurons and triggered neural circuits in a way similar to natural brain activity.

"Other labs have tried to make artificial neurons with organic materials, and they spiked too slowly," Hersam said. "Or they used metal oxides, which are too fast. We are within a temporal range that was not previously demonstrated for artificial neurons. You can see the living neurons respond to our artificial neuron. So, we've demonstrated signals that are not only the right timescale but also the right spike shape to interact directly with living neurons."

Low-Cost, Sustainable Manufacturing and AI Implications

Beyond performance, the new approach offers environmental and practical advantages. The manufacturing process is simple and inexpensive, and the additive printing method places material only where it is needed, reducing waste.

Improving energy efficiency is especially important as artificial intelligence systems grow more demanding. Large data centers already consume vast amounts of power and require significant water for cooling.

"To meet the energy demands of AI, tech companies are building gigawatt data centers powered by dedicated nuclear power plants," Hersam said. "It is evident that this massive power consumption will limit further scaling of computing since it's hard to imagine a next-generation data center requiring 100 nuclear power plants. The other issue is that when you're dissipating gigawatts of power, there's a lot of heat. Because data centers are cooled with water, AI is putting severe stress on the water supply. However you look at it, we need to come up with more energy-efficient hardware for AI."

The study, "Multi-order complexity spiking neurons enabled by printed MoS2 memristive nanosheet networks," was supported by the National Science Foundation.

Materials provided by Northwestern University. Note: Content may be edited for style and length.

#10608. Wendy’s free RPG rewards players with real food for beating bosses and is 15 hours long

Wendy’s has officially launched its free RPG, and somehow, the fast-food chain has created a game longer than some AAA blockbusters, complete with boss battles that reward players with actual food.

When WendyQuest: Legend of the Four Corner Stones was first announced, the biggest surprise was that Wendy’s was making a full-blown, Final Fantasy-style RPG.

As it turns out, the company wasn’t messing around.

According to Wendy’s official website, the free-to-play adventure clocks in at approximately 15 hours of gameplay, featuring over 1,000 lines of dialogue.

For comparison, that’s more than double the length of some AAA campaigns such as Wolverine, which can be finished in 13 hours.

That’s an absurd amount of content for a game about a redheaded burger mascot fighting the forces of frozen beef.

Wendy’s has confirmed that players can unlock real-world food offers by defeating five bosses throughout the adventure.

Each boss provides a chance to earn a reward code redeemable through the Wendy’s app, with prizes including free medium fries, a small Frosty, 10-piece chicken nuggets, a breakfast biscuit or croissant, and even a Dave’s Single burger.

There is, however, a catch. Every food reward requires a separate purchase, and codes are available on a first-come, first-served basis while supplies last. Rewards are also restricted to US residents aged 18 and older.

Still, getting a burger for defeating an RPG boss is certainly a more delicious incentive than unlocking another achievement.

As for the game itself, WendyQuest features turn-based combat, puzzles, and five food-themed regions as players attempt to save the kingdom of Savoria from the villainous Dr. Mechborg.

Whether it’s actually a good RPG remains to be seen, but at least Wendy’s has figured out how to make grinding bosses feel a little more rewarding.

#10605. How to Test Location-Aware APIs with FastAPI and PostGIS

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Have 30-200 Employees? Make $50k-$500k selling your data for AI Training.

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Catch coordinate-order bugs, distance mistakes, boundary cases, and bad spatial assumptions before they reach production.

A location endpoint can return HTTP 200 and still be wrong by kilometers. That is the uncomfortable part of testing geospatial APIs: ordinary API checks tell you whether the request worked, not whether the geography is correct.

Spatial bugs can look harmless in code review. Latitude and longitude get reversed. A radius value is treated as degrees instead of meters. A query works near one city but behaves differently elsewhere because the geometry column uses the wrong type. The JSON is valid, the tests are green, and the map is still wrong.

In this tutorial, you will build a small FastAPI endpoint backed by PostGIS and then test the spatial behavior directly. The examples use deterministic fixtures positioned at known distances, so the tests verify geography rather than only response structure.

The API exposes one endpoint: GET /places/nearby. A client sends longitude, latitude, and a radius in meters. The service returns nearby places as GeoJSON features, ordered by distance.

The useful part is not the endpoint itself. The useful part is the test strategy. We will check coordinate validation, exact distance boundaries, GeoJSON coordinate order, and the database query that turns a radius into a spatial filter.

Use a geography column when the API is answering human-scale distance questions in meters. PostGIS can then calculate on the ellipsoid instead of treating longitude and latitude as flat Cartesian coordinates.

The endpoint below validates longitude, latitude, and radius with Pydantic, then sends a parameterized query to PostgreSQL. The SQL uses ST_DWithin to filter candidates and ST_Distance to return the measured distance.

The API accepts lon and lat as separate named parameters. That removes ambiguity at the HTTP boundary. GeoJSON still uses [longitude, latitude] when the response is created.

Hard-coding two real businesses and hoping their distance is approximately right makes a fragile test. A better approach is to create test points at exact distances from a fixed origin.

PostGIS can generate those fixtures with ST_Project. In the test database, create one point 500 meters away, one 950 meters away, and one 1,500 meters away. Now a 1,000-meter query has an unambiguous expected result: the first two belong in the response and the third does not.

This is the first test that actually proves something spatial. It is not checking that the endpoint returns a list. It is checking that the 1-kilometer contract means what the API says it means.

GeoJSON coordinates are longitude first, latitude second. Many developers naturally think latitude first because that is how coordinates are commonly spoken. A validation layer can catch the most obvious reversal before the database ever sees it.

Longitude must be between -180 and 180. Latitude must be between -90 and 90. That will not catch every reversal, because both numbers may sometimes fall inside both ranges, but it catches a large class of failures and gives clients a useful error instead of a misleading map.

A response can be syntactically valid JSON and still violate the GeoJSON contract. Assert the geometry type and the coordinate order. For a known query point, longitude should appear in element zero and latitude in element one.

Spatial boundaries deserve their own tests. A place at 999 meters should be included in a 1,000-meter search. A place at 1,001 meters should not. A place exactly at the boundary should behave consistently with the predicate you chose.

ST_DWithin includes features whose distance is less than or equal to the supplied threshold, so document that behavior and test it explicitly.

Unit tests are useful for request validation, but mocking the repository layer removes the most important part of this API: PostGIS itself. Keep a small integration-test database and run at least a few tests against the real extension.

That catches mistakes that mocks cannot: geography versus geometry behavior, SRID mismatches, spatial indexes, units, and changes to SQL functions. A location API is one of the cases where a cheap database integration test buys a lot of confidence.

Correctness is only half the story. A radius endpoint that scans every feature will work in development and become painful at production scale. Add a GiST index and inspect the query plan.

The exact plan depends on your data and PostgreSQL version, but you should see the spatial index participating in candidate filtering. Treat an accidental sequential scan on a large production table as a regression worth investigating.

For a real project, I would split the tests into two groups. Fast validation tests run on every commit. PostGIS integration tests run in CI with a temporary PostgreSQL/PostGIS service.

The CI gate should cover invalid coordinates, zero or negative radius values, known-distance fixtures, boundary behavior, GeoJSON output, and at least one query-plan or performance sanity check for larger datasets.

This is more work than asserting status_code == 200, but it tests the thing users actually care about: whether the API's answer is geographically true.

Location APIs fail in ways ordinary web tests are not designed to notice. The response can have the right schema, the database call can succeed, and every field can look reasonable while the location itself is wrong.

The fix is not a giant testing framework. Start with a few spatial invariants you can prove: known distances, valid coordinate ranges, correct GeoJSON order, deterministic boundary behavior, and real PostGIS integration tests. Those checks turn geography from an assumption into something your CI pipeline can verify.
